class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):

            checks  = ((checks[1], checks[2],                        
                      (checks[1] and curr == prev1) or              # <-- conditiion 1
                      (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)) or     
                      (checks[0] and curr == prev1+1 == prev2+2))   # <-- conditiion 3    

        return checks[2]

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:
        if len(nums) < 2:
            return False

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):
            checks = (
                checks[1],
                checks[2],
                (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
                or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
                or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
            )

        return checks[2]
```
Need maybe if len(nums) < 2: return False. But if n=0, `nums[0] == nums[1]` avoided. Good.

Let's maybe produce a slightly more readable fixed code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:
        if len(nums) < 2:
            return False

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):
            two_equal = checks[1] and curr == prev1
            three_equal = checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2
            three_consecutive = checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2

            checks = (checks[1], checks[2], two_equal or three_equal or three_consecutive)

        return checks[2]
```
This is clearer and avoids precedence. But maybe "minimal fix" could be:
```
checks = (
    checks[1],
    checks[2],
    (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
)
```

Let's write final code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:
        if len(nums) < 2:
            return False

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):
            checks = (
                checks[1],
                checks[2],
                (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
                or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
                or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
            )

        return checks[2]
```
Need ensure line `or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)` is inside tuple's third element. Because the tuple has three elements separated by commas:
1. `checks[1]`
2. `checks[2]`
3. `(checks[1] and curr == prev1) or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2) or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)`
Yes, because the third element expression spans until the closing parenthesis of the tuple. The commas after `checks[1]` and `checks[2]` separate. There is no comma after the third expression before closing. Good.

Potential issue: In the tuple literal, the line break after `checks[2],` and then the third expression on next lines. The third expression includes `or` at the beginning of lines. Python implicit line continuation inside parentheses, so okay. Good.

Now, maybe if we want to avoid any ambiguity with `or` and tuple, we could wrap the third expression in parentheses:
```
checks = (
    checks[1],
    checks[2],
    (
        (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
        or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
        or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
    )
)
```
This is even clearer. Let's use that. It's still minimal. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:
        if len(nums) < 2:
            return False

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):
            checks = (
                checks[1],
                checks[2],
                (
                    (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
                    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
                    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
                )
            )

        return checks[2]
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def validPartition(self, nums: List[int]) -> bool:
        if len(nums) < 2:
            return False

        checks = (True, False, nums[0] == nums[1])

        for curr, prev1, prev2 in zip(nums[2:], nums[1:], nums):
            checks = (
                checks[1],
                checks[2],
                (
                    (checks[1] and curr == prev1)
                    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 == prev2)
                    or (checks[0] and curr == prev1 + 1 == prev2 + 2)
                )
            )

        return checks[2]
